% v1 title: Same Shadows, Different Forests: Local Predecessor Structure and Global Dynamics of Collatz-Type Maps
\title[Same Shadows, Different Forests]{Same Shadows, Different Forests:\\ Cycles and Basin Densities of Collatz-Type Maps}

\author[E.\ Sultanow]{Eldar Sultanow\,\orcidlink{0000-0001-5257-2236}}
\address{Eldar Sultanow\\Business Informatics, Processes and Systems\\University of Potsdam\\Karl-Marx-Straße 67, 14482 Potsdam, Germany}
\email{eldar.sultanow@wi.uni-potsdam.de}
\address{Capgemini Deutschland GmbH\\Bahnhofstraße 30, 90402 Nuremberg, Germany}
\email{eldar.sultanow@capgemini.com}

\date{November 2026}

\keywords{Collatz problem, $qx+1$ problem, cycles, parity vectors, rotating binaries, Diophantine approximation, basins of attraction, logarithmic density, first passage, Cayley trees}
\subjclass[2020]{Primary 11B83; Secondary 11Y55, 37P99}

\begin{document}

\begin{abstract}
We study why Collatz-type maps with similar local predecessor statistics can have different global cycles and basin shares. The maps $3x+1$ and $3x-1$ have corresponding local branching and cotree statistics, yet the latter has three known positive cycles. The classical parity-word divisibility criterion, with a meet-in-the-middle search for the longer words, gives an exhaustive census of $1296$ primitive cycles for $3x+c$ with $|c|\le1000$ and parity-word length at most $66$. Using first-passage estimates of Gon\c{c}alves, Greenfeld and Madrid, we prove that every completely invariant set of the accelerated map $T_{3,c}$, for odd $c\ge-1$, has a logarithmic density. In particular, basin and predecessor-set densities exist in this sense. Conditionally on a recent natural-density transport of Allikvere, which carries over to all these maps, they are natural densities. The resulting predecessor potential $h(a)$ satisfies an exact conservation law across the backward levels of a cycle. Gcd classes and multiplicative cosets give further invariant sets of exactly computable density, but do not by themselves determine individual basin densities. We also prove a residue-class \emph{lower bound} for the means of $a\,h(a)$ in terms of the Syracuse law; a matching upper bound and the proposed exact profile remain conjectural. An appendix reduces the missing upper bound, conditionally on the coupling estimates of that proof, to uniform integrability of the predecessor potential of the odd targets; large-scale predecessor counts are consistent with this but cannot decide it. A further appendix shows that the Syracuse profile is $p$-integrable for every $p<2$ as soon as the $L^2$ energy of its unit frequencies stays bounded, which it does numerically, and finds numerically that its largest Fourier coefficients decay like $n^{-1}\,0.943^{\,n}$. Numerically, a parameter-free harmonic backward-mass model predicts $410$ measured basin shares with approximately $3\,\%$ median relative error. Additional conclusions for $3x\pm1$ use recent predecessor-density preprints of Mazur and El Jaouhari. Lean~4 checks the elementary finite statements; the density and Syracuse statements are not machine-certified.
\end{abstract}

%=====================================================================
\section{Introduction}
\label{sec:introduction}
%=====================================================================
Crandall \cite{Crandall_1978} generalized the $3x+1$ sequence by introducing the function $f:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:func_qxplus1}
f(x)=
\begin{cases}
qx+1	&	2\nmid x,\\
x/2		&	\text{otherwise,}
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
for odd $q\geq 3$. More generally, for odd $q\ge1$ and odd $c$ we consider $x\mapsto qx+c$. Since every odd step is followed by at least one halving, we work with the accelerated map
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:T}
T_{q,c}(x)=
\begin{cases}
(qx+c)/2	&	2\nmid x,\\
x/2		&	\text{otherwise,}
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
which has the same cycles as~\eqref{eq:func_qxplus1} (for $c=1$), up to the omitted even members. We call a ``$qx+c$ cycle'' a sequence of distinct positive integers $(x_1,x_2,\ldots,x_n)$ with $T_{q,c}(x_i)=x_{i+1}$ and $T_{q,c}(x_n)=x_1$.

Leslie Davison observed that \emph{circuits}, i.e.\ cycles consisting of one run of odd steps followed by one run of halvings, correspond one-to-one to the solutions $(h,k,l)$ of the Diophantine equation
\begin{equation}
(2^{k+l}-3^k)h=2^l-1,
\label{eq:diophantine_davison}
\end{equation}
see \cite[p.~67]{Lagarias_2010}. Steiner \cite{Steiner_1977} proved that $(h,k,l)=(1,1,1)$ is the only solution, so the trivial cycle is the only circuit of $3x+1$. Simons and de Weger \cite{Simons_deWeger_2005} and Hercher \cite{Hercher_2023} extended this to $m$-cycles (cycles with $m$ runs of odd steps) for $m\le 68$ and $m\le 91$, respectively. The general cycle problem remains open; convergence has been verified for all $n<2^{71}$ \cite{Barina_2021,Barina_2025}.

For $q>3$ cycles are rare. Table~\ref{table:known_cycles} lists the non-trivial cycles of~\eqref{eq:func_qxplus1} for odd $q\le1000$.

\begin{table}[H]
	\centering
	\begin{tabular}{|L|R|}
		\hline
		\thead{\boldsymbol{q}} &
		\thead{\textbf{odd members of the cycle}} \\
		\hline
		5 &
		13,33,83 \\
		5 &
		17,27,43 \\
		181 &
		27,611 \\
		181 &
		35,99 \\
		\hline
	\end{tabular}
	\caption{Non-trivial $qx+1$ cycles for odd $q\leq1000$. Their role for Crandall's conjecture was studied by Franco and Pomerance \cite{Franco_Pomerance_1995}; Koch et al.\ \cite{Koch_2020} confirmed them in a data-science search. A search over all odd $q\le1000$ and all odd starting values below $3000$ finds exactly these, besides the cycles through $1$ that exist for $q=5$ and for $q=2^m-1$.}
	\label{table:known_cycles}
\end{table}

Let $(a_1,a_2,\ldots,a_n)$ be the odd members of a cycle of~\eqref{eq:func_qxplus1} in cyclic order. Then the product
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:condition_cycle_existance}
2^\alpha=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(q+\frac{1}{a_i}\right)
\end{equation}
is a power of two, see for example \cite[p.~327]{Lagarias_2010}. Here $\alpha$ is the number of divisions by $2$ in one period, and $n$ is the number of odd members.

\subsection*{Related work}
Cycles of the maps $3x+d$ have been tabulated before. Lagarias \cite{Lagarias_1990} introduced primitive cycles, whose members are coprime to $d$, and conjectured that every map $3x+d$ has at least one and only finitely many of them. Belaga and Mignotte \cite{Belaga_Mignotte_2000,Belaga_Mignotte_2006} listed the primitive cycles for all $d<20000$, completely as they argue, and Morley \cite{OEIS_A226607} confirmed these lists by following every trajectory with starting value below $10^8$. Belaga and Mignotte also embedded the $3x+1$ conjecture in the $3x+d$ context \cite{Belaga_Mignotte_1998} and described the cycles of all maps $3x+d$ at once, through a duality between the shift $d$ and the members of a cycle \cite{Belaga_Mignotte_DMTCS_2006}. Wegner \cite{Wegner_2021} listed the positive and negative cycles of $3x+k$ for $k\le9997$ reached from starting values $|x_0|\le2\cdot10^7$, using the reduction by common factors of $x$ and $k$. Cox \cite{Cox_2012} modelled the number of cycles per $d$ by a Poisson distribution. These lists are conjectural: a cycle whose basin contains no small starting value would be missed. The census of Section~\ref{sec:zoo} is complete by construction for bounded word length, and it confirms the published lists in that range.

Densities enter through predecessor sets. Wirsching \cite{Wirsching_1998} studied the density of the set of integers that iterate to a fixed integer under $3x+1$ with $3$-adic methods. Tao \cite{Tao_2022} proved that almost all orbits attain almost bounded values, in the sense of logarithmic density. Wegner \cite{Wegner_2021} observed that the oscillation in Tao's mixing estimate is the same for all maps $3x+k$ with $k$ coprime to $6$, and Gon\c{c}alves, Greenfeld and Madrid \cite{GGM_2025} extended Tao's theorem to a class of maps that contains $3x+c$ for odd $c\ge-1$. They also observed numerically that the three cycles of $3x-1$ attract about a third of all integers each, and asked whether such densities exist \cite[Remark~1.7]{GGM_2025}; their question concerns natural densities. Mazur \cite{Mazur_2026} recently upgraded Tao's theorem to natural density, with an explicit logarithmic time bound, by comparing the first-passage laws of natural and logarithmic counting; Allikvere \cite{Allikvere_2026} independently obtained a natural-density analogue of Tao's theorem. Mazur also proved that a positive lower density of the integers reaches $1$ \cite{Mazur_PD_2026} and that, for every $a$ not divisible by $3$, the integers whose orbit reaches $a$ have positive lower natural density \cite{Mazur_Pred_2026}; El Jaouhari \cite{ElJaouhari_2026} proved the analogue for $3x-1$ and deduced Conjecture~A of Applegate and Lagarias. A recent Lean formalisation \cite{CLT_2026} states that these predecessor sets of $3x+1$ have natural densities, together with central limit theorems for the hitting times. Inselmann \cite{Inselmann_2024} obtained descent below $m^\varepsilon$ in natural density for every $\varepsilon>0$, and he proved that the total stopping times $\tau(m)$ of $T_{3,1}$ satisfy $\liminf_{x\to\infty}(x\log x)^{-1}\sum_{m\le x}\tau(m)\ge2/\log(4/3)$, with equality if $\tau(m)=O(\log m)$. Shaik \cite{Shaik_2026} reaches height $(\log m)^{9.99}f(m)$ for every $f\to\infty$ in natural density by counting parity words, after $(2/\log(4/3))\log m+O(\sqrt{\log m\,\log\log m})$ steps of $T_{3,1}$. All these results concern $3x\pm1$; Theorem~\ref{thm:logdens} below is in logarithmic density, but holds for every map $3x+c$ with odd $c\ge-1$.

On the local side, Kleinnijenhuis, Kleinnijenhuis and Aydogan \cite{Kleinnijenhuis_2026} develop a graph-theoretic approach to the $an+b$ conjectures. They represent these maps by Cayley trees with Leftward and Upward edges and three-colour pictures, with a detailed congruence-class bookkeeping that includes cotree densities. Section~\ref{sec:local} adopts this tree perspective.

\subsection*{Two views of the same maps}
These maps admit two descriptions. The \emph{local} view starts from the trivial cycle and grows the tree of all predecessors, organised by congruence classes. It describes how numbers branch, and it is the same for every map in the family: by $x\mapsto -x$, the $3x-1$ map on the positive integers is the $3x+1$ map on the negative integers, which has the three cycles through $-1$, $-5$ and $-17$. Both maps produce the same branching statistics (Proposition~\ref{prop:local}). In the language of Plato's cave, they cast identical shadows. Density arguments built on this view reach ``almost all'' statements \cite{Terras_1976,Tao_2022}, but they cannot decide individual orbits.

The \emph{global} view encodes a cycle by its parity word and asks whether the corresponding rational number is a positive integer. This is decided by the arithmetic of $2^l-q^k$, and it is exactly what separates $3x+1$ from $3x-1$.

The guiding question of this paper is therefore: \emph{what determines the global dynamics of a Collatz-type map when its local predecessor statistics agree with those of another map?} Sections~\ref{sec:local}--\ref{sec:basins} give three complementary answers. The local congruence and branching statistics compared here agree for $3x+1$ and $3x-1$. Cycles are characterised by an exact global divisibility condition. Logarithmic basin densities exist throughout the stated family, but the values vary and are largely unexplained.

\subsection*{Main results}
For $A\subseteq\N$ let $\delta(A)=\lim_{N\to\infty}(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{n<N,\,n\in A}1/n$ be its logarithmic density, if the limit exists. Our main theoretical result separates the existence of basin densities from their values. Theorem~\ref{thm:logdens} states that every set of positive integers that is completely invariant under $T_{3,c}$, with odd $c\ge-1$, has a logarithmic density; in particular, every basin of attraction has one. Existence is therefore common to all maps of the family, whereas the values separate them: numerically, the three basins of $3x-1$ have nearly equal densities, while two cycles of the same type in $3x+5$ attract about $50\,\%$ and $9\,\%$ of all integers. For the Collatz map itself, Corollary~\ref{cor:collatz} shows that the positive integers whose orbit reaches $1$ form a set with a logarithmic density. This does not decide the Collatz conjecture, which asserts that the set is $\N$, but it shows unconditionally that the logarithmic proportion of these integers converges. By a recent natural-counting transport estimate of Mazur \cite{Mazur_2026}, the density is even a natural density (Corollary~\ref{cor:natural}), and by \cite{Mazur_PD_2026} it is positive. The independent transport of Allikvere \cite{Allikvere_2026} carries over to every map $3x+c$; conditionally on it, every completely invariant set and every predecessor set of these maps has a natural density (Theorem~\ref{thm:natural}).

The proof is short. A basin is not changed by the first passage of an orbit below a threshold, so the stabilisation of first passages of Gon\c{c}alves, Greenfeld and Madrid \cite{GGM_2025}, after Tao \cite{Tao_2022}, makes the basin shares of consecutive logarithmic windows converge; two windows of incommensurable ratio then exclude oscillations in $\log\log N$. For $3x+1$, the existence of natural densities of predecessor sets has recently been stated in formalised form \cite{CLT_2026}; Theorem~\ref{thm:logdens} covers the whole family $3x+c$, in logarithmic density. The same argument gives every predecessor set a logarithmic density $h(a)$, and the density of a basin is the sum of $h$ over every level of the backward tree of its cycle (Proposition~\ref{prop:potential}). With the recent positivity results for $3x\pm1$ \cite{Mazur_Pred_2026,ElJaouhari_2026}, every nonempty completely invariant set of these two maps has positive density (Corollary~\ref{cor:dichotomy}); for $3x-1$, the three known cycles attract all integers if and only if their basin densities add up to $1$. How to compute the values from the global dynamics remains the central open problem (Problem~\ref{prob:basins}). Section~\ref{sec:basins} gives two partial answers. An elementary multiplicative invariant gives exact densities for certain invariant residue-class unions, not automatically for their constituent basins (Proposition~\ref{prop:cosets}); for a prime $p\equiv\pm1\pmod{24}$, for example, the Legendre symbol $(n/p)$ is constant along the orbits of $3x+p$. And the harmonic mass of the backward tree of a cycle, a heuristic quantity computed from the cycle alone, predicts the measured densities of $410$ cycles with a median deviation of about $5\,\%$ after normalisation within each map, and of $3\,\%$ in absolute terms with the explicit constant $3/\log(4/3)$ (Figure~\ref{fig:predictor}, Conjecture~\ref{conj:formula}).

%=====================================================================
\section{Contributions}
\label{sec:contributions}
%=====================================================================
The results of this paper have different logical statuses: theorems with proofs
in the present manuscript, deductions that use recent external results,
exhaustive computation within stated bounds, and conjectures supported by
numerical tests. Table~\ref{table:contributions} distinguishes these levels
and compares each contribution with the closest preceding work. In particular,
we do not claim a proof of universal convergence or an exact formula for
individual basin shares, and the natural densities for general $c$ rest on
the preprint \cite{Allikvere_2026}, which is not yet refereed. ``Exact bounded
computation'' asserts completeness only within the stated limits, and numerical
agreement is not a proof of a limiting density.

& Kahane--Peyri\`ere inequality: the profile lies in $L^p$ for every $p<2$ if the unit-frequency energy $E_n$ is bounded; exact second-moment identity; logarithmic chart, in which $E_n$ is a weighted unit-frequency energy; the singular set of the correlations consists of the intertwiners of inverse Syracuse words and is avoided by the maps that enter $E_n$ (Appendix~\ref{sec:syracuse-lp}). A first-hit decomposition reduces the boundedness of $E_n$ to a mixing statement (Corollary~\ref{cor:energy-bootstrap}). Exact laws up to $n=20$ and Monte Carlo up to $n=80$: $E_n\to0.475$, with the slowest convergence at the fixed point $-1$; largest Fourier coefficient $\approx n^{-1}0.943^n$ (Runs~L--O).
& \emph{Proof of the reduction; numerical evidence for its hypothesis and for exponential decay}. \\

& Empirical multi-basin shares in \cite{GGM_2025}; no general density formula.

& \emph{Numerical evidence; conjecture}. \\

& Renewal estimates for the model walk (Lemma~\ref{lem:model}) and the transfer of Lemma~\ref{lem:reduction}.
& Calibration of the clipped mass; exponential tightness of $\Phi_X/X$ up to an exceptional set of logarithmic density $O((\log X)^{-\varepsilon_0})$; whether $T_X=o(X)$ then depends only on the mass carried by this set, which is not controlled; two-level return certificates and a fixed-modulus obstruction (research note); $T_X=o(X)$ follows if the stopped potential of the odd targets is uniformly integrable, and $J_X=o(X)$ if the full potential is (Appendix~\ref{sec:tail-programme}).
& \emph{Proof under the uniformities of Lemmas~\ref{lem:reduction}--\ref{lem:model}; $T_X=o(X)$ and $J_X=o(X)$ open}. \\

The unifying object is the \emph{predecessor potential} $h(a)$.
For a cycle $\mathcal C$, Proposition~\ref{prop:potential} gives the exact
conservation law
\[
  \delta(\mathcal C)=\sum_{x\in L_d(\mathcal C)} h(x)
  \qquad(d\ge0).
\]
The harmonic backward-mass predictor replaces these unknown exact weights
by an explicit multiple of $1/x$; this replacement is \emph{conjectural}, not
part of the conservation theorem. The new theoretical connection is
Theorem~\ref{thm:lower}, which proves the \emph{lower} half of the proposed
Syracuse residue-class profile of $a\,h(a)$. The matching upper bound
\eqref{eq:upper}, and hence the predicted profile and absolute basin-density
formula, remain open (Corollary~\ref{cor:remains},
Conjectures~\ref{conj:profile} and~\ref{conj:formula}). The measurements in
Tables~\ref{table:syracuse} and~\ref{table:convergence} provide independent
numerical tests of these conjectures, but do not establish their limits.
Appendix~\ref{sec:tail-programme} splits the deficit in~\eqref{eq:upper} into a
clipping tail and a return term and, conditionally on the coupling estimates of
the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:lower}, reduces both to uniform
integrability of the potential of the odd targets
(Propositions~\ref{prop:potential-domination} and~\ref{prop:return-domination}), which the counts of Run~K support
but cannot decide. Appendix~\ref{sec:syracuse-lp} turns to the Syracuse profile itself: its $p$-integrability for $p<2$ follows from a bounded $L^2$ energy of the unit frequencies, which we observe numerically, as we do an exponential decay of its largest Fourier coefficients. It also reduces the bounded energy to a quantitative non-resonance between the correlation maps and the intertwiners of inverse Syracuse words, whose exact form it proves, and it reduces the main term of the energy to a mixing statement for the class distribution of a renormalisation walk. All computations are labelled Run~A to Run~O;
Table~\ref{table:runs} lists them with links to their data.


\subsection*{Machine-checked finite core and its scope}
A companion Lean~4 development formalises the accelerated integer-valued
$3x+c$ map, its immediate predecessors for odd $c$, the recursion for
hitting a target within a fixed number of steps, and a finite weighted
hitting-mass identity for arbitrary finite samples. The separately checked
modules \texttt{Clipping.lean} and \texttt{ReturnPairs.lean} certify
finite truncation inequalities and the chronological return-pair identity. The links in the last row of Table~\ref{table:contributions} point to the
individual declarations, among them the finite return certificates and explicit
congruence witnesses. These statements are checked by the
\href{https://github.com/Sultanow/collatz/actions/workflows/collatz-lean.yml}{GitHub Actions workflow}
with Lean~4 and Mathlib. It builds every module, so that every declaration
passes the Lean kernel; it rejects \texttt{sorry}, \texttt{admit},
\texttt{native\_decide} and \texttt{axiom} declarations; and it fails unless
each theorem listed in \texttt{AxiomAudit.lean} depends at most on the axioms
\texttt{propext}, \texttt{Quot.sound} and \texttt{Classical.choice}. An
optional job replays the build in an independent kernel (\texttt{lake check}).
The links point to commit \href{https://github.com/Sultanow/collatz/commit/ee5a5b4}{\texttt{ee5a5b4}}, for which both jobs of the workflow, including the independent replay, ran successfully.

This formal verification is deliberately \emph{limited to finite dynamics}.
In particular, it does not certify Theorem~\ref{thm:logdens},
Theorem~\ref{thm:natural},
Proposition~\ref{prop:potential}'s logarithmic-density assertions,
Lemma~\ref{lem:model}, Theorem~\ref{thm:lower}, or the first-passage calibration and
tightness (Propositions~\ref{prop:clipped-calibration} and~\ref{prop:tightness}). Their proofs and external dependencies require separate
mathematical and, potentially, formal verification.

\FloatBarrier

%=====================================================================
\section{Local structure: one forest, several trees}
\label{sec:local}
%=====================================================================
The local view grows the tree of all predecessors of a cycle. For the Collatz map this tree is usually restricted to the \emph{branching numbers}, the even numbers with an odd predecessor, and drawn as a Cayley tree with \emph{Leftward} edges through an odd predecessor and \emph{Upward} edges by doubling \cite{Sultanow_graph_2020}. The congruence-class bookkeeping of these trees for general $an+b$ maps, including their \emph{cotree densities}, is developed in detail by Kleinnijenhuis et al.\ \cite{Kleinnijenhuis_2026}. In this section we record two facts that hold for every map of the family alike.

Let $c$ be coprime to $6$. A \emph{branching number} is an even $n\in\N$ such that $(n-c)/3$ is an odd integer not divisible by $3$; let $S_c$ be the set of branching numbers. For $m\in S_c$ define the \emph{Leftward child} $L(m)=2^p\,(m-c)/3$ and the \emph{Upward child} $U(m)=2^r m$, where $p,r\ge1$ are minimal with $L(m),U(m)\in S_c$. Both exist because $2$ is a primitive root modulo $9$. Every branching number has exactly one parent, either as a Leftward or as an Upward child: the maps $L,U:S_c\to S_c$ are injective, and $S_c$ is the disjoint union of $L(S_c)$ and $U(S_c)$ \cite{Sultanow_graph_2020,Kleinnijenhuis_2026}. The parent of $n$ is found by halving $n$ until the result is either a branching number $m$, the Upward parent, or an odd number $o$, in which case $3o+c$ is the Leftward parent.

For $c=\pm1$, this halving stops after at most four steps, so whether $n$ is a Leftward or an Upward child depends only on $n\bmod 2^4\cdot18=288$. For $3x+1$, Kleinnijenhuis et al.\ count $27$ Leftward and $5$ Upward classes modulo $288$ \cite[(3.73), (3.79)]{Kleinnijenhuis_2026}, which gives the cotree density
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:forest_density}
d\bigl(L(S_c)\bigr)+d\bigl(U(S_c)\bigr)=\frac{27}{288}+\frac{5}{288}=\frac{2}{18}=d(S_c).
\end{equation}
The same counts hold for $3x-1$, whose Upward classes are the negatives of those of $3x+1$ modulo $288$ (Table~\ref{table:mirror}). The identity~\eqref{eq:forest_density} gives the density of the whole forest $S_c$, not of the tree of the trivial cycle: for $3x-1$ that tree contains only about a third of all integers (Section~\ref{sec:basins}).

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
 & $3x+1$ ($c=1$) & $3x-1$ ($c=-1$) \\
\midrule
branching classes mod $18$ & $\{4,16\}$ & $\{2,14\}$ \\
Leftward classes mod $288$ & $27$ & $27$ \\
Upward classes mod $288$ & $\{16,88,160,232,256\}$ & $\{32,56,128,200,272\}$ \\
forest density $d(S_c)$ & $27/288+5/288=2/18$ & $27/288+5/288=2/18$ \\
cycles with word length $\le 66$ & $1$ & $3$ \\
basins (trees of the forest) & $1$ (verified for $n<2^{71}$) & $3$, empirical: $32.7$, $32.5$, $34.8\,\%$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{The mirror pair $3x\pm1$: identical local structure (upper rows; the Upward classes are negatives of each other modulo $288$), different global structure (lower rows; Sections~\ref{sec:zoo} and~\ref{sec:basins}).}
\label{table:mirror}
\end{table}

\subsection*{Local indistinguishability}
Let $\iota(n)=-n$. Then $T_{3,-1}=\iota\circ T_{3,1}\circ\iota$ on $\Z$. We call a set $A\subseteq\N$ \emph{locally defined} for a map $T$ if, for some fixed $m$ and modulus $M$, membership of $n$ in $A$ depends only on the residues of $n,T(n),\ldots,T^m(n)$ modulo $M$. The branching numbers, the Leftward and Upward children and the cotree classes are locally defined, and so is, for every word $B$ of length $m$, the set of $n$ whose first $m$ parities form $B$.

\begin{proposition}[Local indistinguishability]
\label{prop:local}
Let $A\subseteq\N$ be locally defined for $T_{3,1}$ by residue conditions $R$, and let $A^-\subseteq\N$ be the set defined for $T_{3,-1}$ by the negated conditions $-R$. Then $A$ and $A^-$ are periodic sets with the same natural density.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For $T=T_{3,\pm1}$, the residue of $T(n)$ modulo $M$ depends only on $n$ modulo $2M$, hence the residue of $T^i(n)$ modulo $M$ depends only on $n$ modulo $2^iM$. Thus $A=\{n\in\N: n\bmod 2^mM\in Q\}$ for a set $Q$ of residues, and the same condition defines a periodic set $\tilde A\subseteq\Z$ for $T_{3,1}$ on all of $\Z$. Since $T_{3,-1}(n)=-T_{3,1}(-n)$, we have $n\in A^-$ if and only if $-n\in\tilde A$, that is, $A^-=\{n\in\N:-n\bmod 2^mM\in Q\}$. Both sets have density $|Q|/(2^mM)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
\label{cor:local}
A statement about $T_{3,1}$ that is formulated only in terms of the densities of locally defined sets holds if and only if the corresponding statement holds for $T_{3,-1}$. In particular, local branching statistics determine neither the number of cycles nor basin membership: $3x+1$ has one known cycle and $3x-1$ has three (Section~\ref{sec:zoo}), and Table~\ref{table:classes} shows that membership in a basin is not a function of any residue class.
\end{corollary}

Corollary~\ref{cor:local} limits proof strategies that use only local branching statistics, such as cotree densities. It does not exclude proofs that combine the predecessor tree with global information that differs between $3x+1$ and $3x-1$, such as the size of the numbers involved or the arithmetic of $2^l-3^k$ (Section~\ref{sec:words}).

\subsection*{Residue classes do not decide membership}
Which tree of the forest a branching number belongs to is not a property of its residue class, for none of the moduli $18$, $288$ and $4608$ used in this kind of bookkeeping. Table~\ref{table:classes} shows this for $3x-1$, where all trees are known: members of the same class lie in different trees, in both directions. A check of the representatives $\ell=0$ can therefore refute a conjecture (the number $14$ refutes it for $3x-1$), but it cannot confirm one. For $3x+1$, the representatives $4$ and $16$ lie in the tree of the root; whether all members of $18\ell+4$ and $18\ell+16$ do is the Collatz conjecture itself. Section~\ref{sec:basins} shows more: for $3x-1$, $3x+5$ and $3x+13$, residue classes modulo powers of $2$ and $3$ do not even shift the basin shares measurably.

\midrule
$18\ell+14$ & $32$ ($\ell=1$) & $14$ ($\ell=0$) & through $5$ \\
$18\ell+2$ & $2$ ($\ell=0$, the root) & $20$ ($\ell=1$) & through $5$ \\
$288\ell+2$ & $2,290,578,866$ ($\ell\le3$) & $1154$ ($\ell=4$) & through $17$ \\

%=====================================================================
\section{Parity words and cycles}
\label{sec:words}
%=====================================================================
Fix odd $q\ge1$ and odd $c$, and write $T=T_{q,c}$. A \emph{word} $B=b_0b_1\cdots b_{l-1}\in\{0,1\}^l$ prescribes the parities of $l$ consecutive iterates. Its \emph{type} is $(l,k)$ with $k=b_0+\cdots+b_{l-1}$, the number of odd steps; we write
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:D}
D=D(l,k)=2^l-q^k .
\end{equation}
Let $0\le p_1<p_2<\cdots<p_k\le l-1$ be the positions of the ones in $B$; the position $0$ refers to the first step. The rotating-binary function of Cox et al.\ \cite{Cox_2021}, adapted from Halbeisen and Hungerbühler \cite{Halbeisen_Hungerbuehler_1997}, is
\begin{equation}
z_q(B) = \sum_{j=1}^{k}q^{\,k-j}\,2^{p_j}.
\label{eq:z}
\end{equation}
Let $\sigma B=b_1\cdots b_{l-1}b_0$ denote the left rotation of $B$, and let $\Zt$ be the ring of rationals with odd denominator. The following correspondence is classical \cite{Boehm_Sontacchi_1978,Lagarias_1990}; we state it in the notation of rotating binaries.

\begin{lemma}[Cycles from words; B\"ohm--Sontacchi, Lagarias]
\label{lem:words}
Let $B$ be a word of type $(l,k)$ with $D\neq0$, and put
\[
x_B=\frac{c\,z_q(B)}{D}\in\Zt .
\]
Then $x_B$ is a periodic point of $T$ (extended to $\Zt$) whose parity sequence is $BBB\cdots$, and it is the only such point in $\Zt$. The orbit of $x_B$ is $x_B, x_{\sigma B}, x_{\sigma^2B},\ldots$. Consequently:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
	\item $T$ has an integer cycle with parity word $B$ if and only if $D\mid c\,z_q(B)$;
	\item for $k\ge1$ this cycle consists of positive integers if and only if $c\,D>0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

It rests on the identity $T_B(x)=(q^kx+c\,z_q(B))/2^l$ for the composition $T_B$ of the branches $x/2$ and $(qx+c)/2$ along $B$, whose unique fixed point is $x_B$.

Every positive integer cycle of $T$ arises in this way from the word of its parities, and its members are obtained by rotating the word. This is the precise sense in which cycles are \emph{rotating binaries}. The reverse direction, asking for which maps a given word yields a cycle, is also classical: it is Lagarias' theory of rational cycles \cite{Lagarias_1990} and the shift--member duality of Belaga and Mignotte \cite[Theorem~4]{Belaga_Mignotte_DMTCS_2006}.

For example, for $q=5$ the word $B=111110000000$ of type $(12,5)$ has $D=2^{12}-5^5=971$. With $c=971$, $x_B=z_5(B)=1031$, and the twelve rotations of $B$ give the cycle of $5x+971$ through $1031$; the ones of $B$ mark its odd members $1031,3063,8143,20843,52593$.

\paragraph{Words with $D=c$} If $c=2^l-q^k>0$, then $D=c$, and Lemma~\ref{lem:words} gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:D_equals_c}
x_B=z_q(B)\qquad\text{for every word $B$ of type $(l,k)$.}
\end{equation}
Every such word therefore yields a positive integer cycle of $T_{q,c}$, with members $z_q(\sigma^iB)$, and each necklace (rotation class) of type $(l,k)$ gives one cycle. This explains the cycles of Table~\ref{table:non_q1_cycles}, where $7=2^5-5^2$, $103=2^7-5^2$, $3=2^7-5^3$ and $131=2^8-5^3$, and the cycle families of $3x+5$ ($5=2^5-3^3$) and $3x+13$ ($13=2^8-3^5$) in Section~\ref{sec:zoo}.

\begin{table}[H]
	\centering
	\begin{tabular}{|L|L|L|L|L|R|}
		\hline
		\thead{\textbf{map}} &
		\thead{\boldsymbol{B_{min}}} & 
        \thead{\boldsymbol{B_{max}}} & 
        \thead{\boldsymbol{r}} & 
        \thead{\boldsymbol{c}} & 
        \thead{\textbf{cycle}} \\
		\hline
		5x + 7 & 00101 & 10100 & 2 & 2^5-5^2 & 9,26,13,36,18 \\
        5x + 103 & 0001001 & 1001000 & 3 & 2^7-5^2 & 13,84,42,21,104,52,26 \\
        5x + 3 & 0010101 & 1010100 & 2 & 2^7-5^3 & 61,154,77,194,97,244,122 \\
        5x + 131 & 00100101 & 10100100 & 5 & 2^8-5^3 & 77,258,129,388,194,97,308,154 \\
		\hline
	\end{tabular}
	\caption{Cycles determined via binary rotation \cite{Gupta_etal_2022}; $r$ is the left-rotational distance from $B_{\min}$ to $B_{\max}$, and $c=2^l-5^k$, see~\eqref{eq:D_equals_c}. The notebook is available on GitHub \cite{sultanow2024}.}
	\label{table:non_q1_cycles}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Mirror and $D=1$} The conjugation $x\mapsto-x$ gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:mirror}
T_{q,-c}(x)=-T_{q,c}(-x),
\end{equation}
so the cycles of $3x-1$ are the negatives of the cycles of $3x+1$ on the negative integers. By Lemma~\ref{lem:words}(b) they come from words with $D<0$: the three cycles of $3x-1$ through $1$, $5$ and $17$ have the words $1$, $110$ and $11110111000$, with $D=-1$, $-1$ and $-139$ (since $2^3<3^2$ and $2^{11}<3^7$). If $q=2^m-1$, the word $10^{m-1}$ has $D=2^m-q=1$ and $z_q=1$, so $x_B=c$; for $c=1$ the number $1$ lies on a cycle. This is the power-of-two root $c=q+1=2^m$ of the family $a=2^m-1$ studied in \cite{Kleinnijenhuis_2026}.

Two further elementary remarks, on the case $q=1$ and on a product identity for cycles of the same type, are collected in Appendix~\ref{appx:cycles}.

%=====================================================================
\section{The decoded zoo: cycles live where \texorpdfstring{$2^l\approx q^k$}{2\^{}l ≈ q\^{}k}}
\label{sec:zoo}
%=====================================================================
By Lemma~\ref{lem:words}, the positive integer cycles of $T_{q,c}$ with word length $l\le L$ can be found exhaustively. One enumerates all words $B$ of length $l\le L$ with $c\,D>0$ and tests whether $D\mid c\,z_q(B)$. For $3x+c$ with $c>0$ and $L=30$ these are $1.96\cdot10^9$ words, which takes a few minutes on a single core. The program and its outputs are available in \cite{Sultanow_collatz_07}.

\begin{proposition}[Census]
\label{prop:census}
The cycles listed in Table~\ref{table:zoo} are all positive integer cycles of $T_{q,c}$ whose parity word has length at most $L$, with $L=66$ for $3x+1$, $3x-1$, $3x+5$, $3x+13$ (by Proposition~\ref{prop:rational}), $L=28$ for $5x+1$ and $L=40$ for $181x+1$.
\end{proposition}

\subsection*{Near-coincidences}
In every row of Table~\ref{table:zoo} with a non-trivial cycle, $|D|$ is small compared with $2^l$. For example, $2^{15}-181^2=32768-32761=7$, $2^7-5^3=128-125=3$ and $|D|/2^l=0.038$ for the type $(27,17)$ of $3x+5$. Equivalently, $l/k$ is a good rational approximation of $\log_2 q$:
\[
\begin{array}{rcll}
15/2 &=& 7.5 & \approx \log_2 181 = 7.49985,\\
7/3 &=& 2.333 & \approx \log_2 5 = 2.32193,\\
27/17,\ 8/5,\ 11/7 &=& 1.588,\ 1.600,\ 1.571 & \approx \log_2 3 = 1.58496 .
\end{array}
\]
For $3x-1$ the approximations come from below ($2^l<3^k$), in accordance with~\eqref{eq:mirror}. The heuristic count $E$ explains the pattern. A small $|D|$ makes the divisibility $D\mid c\,z_q(B)$ likely, while a large $|D|$ needs an astronomical number of words.

\subsection*{The near miss of \texorpdfstring{$3x+1$}{3x+1}}
The type $(8,5)$ illustrates where the $3x+1$ problem is decided.

\begin{corollary}
\label{cor:near_miss}
The $3x+1$ map has no cycle of type $(8,5)$, and the seven cycles of $3x+13$ of this type are exactly the seven candidates.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By Lemma~\ref{lem:words}, a $3x+1$ cycle of type $(8,5)$ requires $13=2^8-3^5$ to divide $z_3(B)$ for a word $B$ of that type. For $c=13$ we have $D=c$, so by~\eqref{eq:D_equals_c} the values $z_3(B)$ are exactly the members of the seven cycles of $3x+13$ of type $(8,5)$. Their minima $211,227,251,259,283,287,319$ are congruent to $3,6,4,12,10,1,7$ modulo $13$. Since $T_{3,13}$ maps non-multiples of $13$ to non-multiples of $13$, no member of these cycles is divisible by $13$.
\end{proof}

The heuristic expectation for this type is $E=7/13\approx0.54$; the actual count is $0$. Belaga and Mignotte call $2^l-3^k$ a Collatz number and the values $z_3(B)$ of the words of type $(l,k)$ that begin with an odd step its corona. In their language, Corollary~\ref{cor:near_miss} states that the Collatz number $2^8-3^5=13$ divides no member of its corona; they showed that the absence of non-trivial positive $3x+1$ cycles is equivalent to this property for every non-trivial type \cite[Theorem~5]{Belaga_Mignotte_DMTCS_2006}. For longer types, the methods of Steiner, Simons--de Weger and Hercher \cite{Steiner_1977,Simons_deWeger_2005,Hercher_2023} combine lower bounds for $|l\log2-k\log3|$ from Baker's theory with the verification limit. These bounds show that $D$ is too large for the divisibility to occur in cycles with few runs. Whatever proves the absence of cycles must see this arithmetic. The local structure of Section~\ref{sec:local} does not see it, because it is identical for $3x+1$ and $3x+13$ up to scaling.

\subsection*{All maps at once: rational cycles}
We now take the reverse perspective: instead of asking which cycles a given map has, we ask for which maps a given parity word yields a cycle. This is the point of view of Lagarias' rational cycles \cite{Lagarias_1990} and of the shift--member duality of Belaga and Mignotte \cite[Section~7]{Belaga_Mignotte_DMTCS_2006}. Lemma~\ref{lem:words} answers the question for every word. Let $d_0$ be the denominator of $z_3(B)/D$ in lowest terms. Then $x_B=c\,z_3(B)/D$ is a positive integer if and only if $c=\operatorname{sign}(D)\,m\,d_0$ with $m$ odd, and the cycle is $m$ times the \emph{primitive} cycle of $3x+c_0$, $c_0=\operatorname{sign}(D)\,d_0$. These are Lagarias' rational cycles of $3x+1$ with denominator $d_0$ \cite{Lagarias_1990}. A single pass over all words therefore lists the primitive cycles of all maps with $|c_0|\le C$. The denominator comes from the continued-fraction expansion of $z_3(B)/|D|$, which can be stopped as soon as a convergent has a denominator larger than $C$.

\begin{proposition}[Rational cycles]
\label{prop:rational}
For word length $l\le66$ there are exactly $1296$ primitive cycles of the maps $3x+c_0$ with $|c_0|\le1000$: $947$ with $c_0>0$ and $349$ with $c_0<0$, for $257$ distinct values of $|c_0|$. Every cycle of a map $3x+c$ with $|c|\le1000$ and word length at most $66$ is an odd multiple of one of them. Of these cycles, $1097$ have $l\le40$: $763$ with $c_0>0$ and $334$ with $c_0<0$, for $199$ distinct values of $|c_0|$.
\end{proposition}

The computation for $l\le40$ (Run~A; all runs are listed in Table~\ref{table:runs}) checked all $2\,199\,023\,255\,510$ words in $512$ shards of about six minutes each on the HPC cluster of the EDIH Hamburg, with a parity check of every reported cycle. The program was validated against an independent implementation in exact rational arithmetic (for $l\le16$ and $|c_0|\le50$) and against the single-map census for $3x\pm1$, $3x+5$ and $3x+13$. The $1.5\cdot10^{20}$ words with $41\le l\le66$ cannot be checked one by one. For them (Run~P), a word of type $(l,k)$ is split as $B=B_1B_2$ with $|B_1|=h=\lfloor l/2\rfloor$, so that $z_3(B)=3^{k_2}z_3(B_1)+2^hz_3(B_2)$ by~\eqref{eq:z}, where $k_2$ is the number of ones in $B_2$. A primitive cycle with $|c_0|=d_0\le C$ requires $(|D|/d_0)\mid z_3(B)$. Every divisor $d_0\le C$ of $|D|$ divides a divisor $d\le C$ that is maximal for divisibility, so it suffices that $M\mid z_3(B)$ with $M=|D|/L$, where $L$ is the least common multiple of a group of such maximal divisors. The search sorts the values $3^{k_2}z_3(B_1)$ and $-2^hz_3(B_2)$ modulo $M$, matches them, and checks every match in exact arithmetic. It covered all $1391$ types with $41\le l\le66$ in $46$ core-hours, with at most $1.2\cdot10^9$ half-words per list, and it reproduces Run~A for $l\le24$.

\paragraph{Comparison with the published tables} For each of the $333$ values $0<k<1000$ with $\gcd(k,6)=1$, the primitive cycles of $3x+k$ listed by Belaga, Mignotte and Morley \cite{Belaga_Mignotte_2006,OEIS_A226607} that have at most $66$ elements coincide exactly with those of Proposition~\ref{prop:rational}; there are $947$ of them. The other $278$ listed cycles have between $67$ and $426$ elements, and for $91$ of these maps every known primitive cycle is longer than $66$. Proposition~\ref{prop:rational} therefore turns the conjecturally complete tables into a theorem for all cycles with at most $66$ elements and $k<1000$.

\paragraph{Heuristic} If $z_3(B)$ were equidistributed modulo $|D|$, the reduced denominator of $z_3(B)/|D|$ would equal a divisor $e$ of $|D|$ with probability $\varphi(e)/|D|$. A primitive cycle corresponds to one aperiodic necklace of words, so the expected number of primitive cycles of type $(l,k)$ with $|c_0|\le C$ is
\[
E_C(l,k)=L(l,k)\,\frac{S_C(D)}{|D|},\qquad S_C(D)=\sum_{e\,\mid\,|D|,\ e\le C}\varphi(e),
\]
where $L(l,k)$ is the number of aperiodic binary necklaces of type $(l,k)$. If all divisors of $|D|$ are at most $C$, then $S_C(D)=|D|$, every necklace yields a cycle, and $E_C$ is exact; for example, $D(10,5)=781=11\cdot71$ gives all $25$ necklaces. Summed over $l\le40$, the heuristic predicts $1148.7$ primitive cycles against $1097$ observed, and it reproduces the counts per word length including the peaks (Figure~\ref{fig:cycles}); for longer words it overestimates them (see below). For $l\le40$, the agreement has a deterministic and a statistical part. For the $44$ types with $|D|\le1000$, all divisors of $|D|$ are admissible, every necklace yields a cycle, and the $227$ cycles of these types are predicted exactly. For the other $116$ types with $E_{1000}\ge1$, the heuristic predicts $879.8$ cycles and we observe $839$; the remaining types with $E_{1000}<1$ contribute $41.9$ expected and $31$ observed cycles. The deviations have a systematic and a statistical part (Run~J). The deficit comes from the types with $l$ and $k$ even and $D>0$: for these $46$ types the heuristic predicts $379.8$ cycles and we observe $329$, whereas the $49$ types with $\gcd(l,k)=1$ give $348$ cycles against $338.2$. For even $l$ and $k$, $D=(2^{l/2}-3^{k/2})(2^{l/2}+3^{k/2})$, and every periodic word $B=B'B'$ satisfies $P\mid z_3(B)$ for $P=2^{l/2}+3^{k/2}$, because $z_3(B'B')=P\,z_3(B')$. These words give no primitive cycles of type $(l,k)$, and the aperiodic words are not equidistributed modulo $P$: over the $38$ types with $l\le28$, $D>0$ and at least five expected multiples, only $0.61$ times the expected proportion $1/P$ of the aperiodic words have $P\mid z_3(B)$; for the $9$ such types with $D<0$ the factor is $1.40$. The heuristic thus fails for the algebraic factors of $2^l-3^k$. In addition, the counts scatter less than a Poisson model allows: over the $116$ types, $\sum(O-E)^2/E=71.4$, a dispersion index of $0.62$, and the index is $0.64$ for the coprime types alone. It depends on the bound $C$, however: an exact count of all words with $l\le30$ gives the index $0.55$ for $C=1000$ and $1.09$ for $C=2\cdot10^4$.

The algebraic factor $P$ can be analysed exactly by splitting the word in halves.

\begin{lemma}[Half-word collisions]
\label{lem:halfword}
Let $l=2h$ and $k=2\kappa$ be even, $P=2^h+3^\kappa$, and write a word of type $(l,k)$ as $B=B_1B_2$ with half-words of length $h$ that contain $k_1$ and $k_2$ ones.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item $z_3(B)=3^{k_2}z_3(B_1)+2^hz_3(B_2)\equiv3^{k_2}z_3(B_1)-3^\kappa z_3(B_2)\pmod P$.
\item The condition $P\mid z_3(B)$ is invariant under rotation, and every word has a balanced rotation, $k_1=k_2=\kappa$. For a balanced word, $P\mid z_3(B)$ if and only if $z_3(B_2)-z_3(B_1)\in P\Z$.
\item On half-words with $\kappa$ ones, $z_3$ is injective. Hence $z_3(B_2)=z_3(B_1)$ exactly for the periodic words $B=B'B'$. Every aperiodic necklace with $P\mid z_3$ has a balanced rotation with $z_3(B_2)-z_3(B_1)=mP$, where $1\le|m|<2^{h-\kappa}3^\kappa/P$, that is $|m|<(3/2)^\kappa$ if $D>0$ and $|m|<2^{h-\kappa}$ if $D<0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) Split the sum~\eqref{eq:z} after the $k_1$-th one, and use $2^h\equiv-3^\kappa\pmod P$. (b) By Lemma~\ref{lem:words} with $c=1$, $x_{\sigma B}$ is $x_B/2$ or $(3x_B+1)/2$. So $z_3(\sigma B)$ is $z_3(B)/2$ or $(3z_3(B)+D)/2$, a unit multiple of $z_3(B)$ modulo $P$, because $P\mid D$. The number of ones in a cyclic window of length $h$ changes by at most $1$ per shift, and the windows starting at $s$ and $s+h$ hold $k$ ones together; hence some window holds exactly $\kappa$ ones. For a balanced word, (a) gives $z_3(B)\equiv3^\kappa(z_3(B_1)-z_3(B_2))$, and $3$ is a unit modulo $P$. (c) The $2$-adic valuation of $z_3(B_i)$ is the position of the first one, whose coefficient $3^{\kappa-1}$ is odd. Subtracting this term and repeating recovers all positions. The values of $z_3$ on half-words with $\kappa$ ones lie between $3^\kappa-2^\kappa$ and $2^{h-\kappa}(3^\kappa-2^\kappa)$.
\end{proof}

So divisibility by $P$ is an additive equation between two half-word values, and the equidistribution heuristic misses two of its features (Run~J, continued). Count the ordered pairs of distinct balanced half-words with $z_3(B_2)-z_3(B_1)\in P\Z$. The heuristic expects $(n^2-n)/P$ of them, $n=\binom h\kappa$. First, the slot $m=0$ is empty by (c), so the many pairs with $|z_3(B_2)-z_3(B_1)|<P/2$ cannot contribute. For the $33$ types with $D>0$, $l\le28$ and at least five expected solutions, this slot carries $39\,\%$ of the heuristic expectation. Second, the difference has non-uniform local densities: $z_3$ of a half-word is never divisible by $3$, and its $2$-adic valuation is the position of its first one. With the exact densities modulo $2^6$ and $3^4$, the prediction becomes $0.389$ of the heuristic count, against $0.334$ observed (residual factor $0.86$). For $D<0$ the half-words have few zeros, and the counts no longer depend on $h$. Half-words with $3$, $4$, $5$ and $6$ zeros give $0$, $16$ (except $22$ at $h=13$), $104$ to $124$, and $544$ to $606$ solutions for every $h\le21$, while the heuristic expectation tends to $0$. A run of $L$ ones starting at position $a$, with $U$ ones above it, contributes $3^U2^a(3^L-2^L)$ to $z_3$. So a half-word with $z_0$ zeros has $z_3$ equal to a signed sum of at most $2(z_0+1)$ numbers $2^\alpha3^\beta$, and the collision equation becomes an $S$-unit equation with $S=\{2,3\}$ and a bounded number of terms. Such equations have only finitely many non-degenerate solutions up to proportionality \cite{Evertse_1984}, which fits the constant counts. The lemma also gives an exact meet-in-the-middle count of $P\mid z_3$ from the $\binom h\kappa$ half-word values; the census of Run~P uses the same split with other moduli.


\paragraph{Longer words} Of the $199$ primitive cycles with $41\le l\le66$ (Run~P), $184$ have $c_0>0$ and $15$ have $c_0<0$; they add $58$ values of $|c_0|$, and none of them belongs to $3x\pm1$, $3x+5$ or $3x+13$. They include the three cycles beyond $l=40$ that a search over the starting values $n<10^5$ of the maps of Section~\ref{sec:basins} finds: the cycle of $3x+23$ through $41$ ($l=43$) and two cycles of $3x+29$ through $3811$ and $7055$ ($l=65$, $k=41$). The last two sit exactly at the convergent $65/41$ of $\log_2 3$, the one after $19/12$. The same type gives seven primitive cycles of $3x+551$, with minima between $120\,665$ and $212\,665$. Since $D(65,41)=19\cdot29\cdot17021\cdot44835377399$, the heuristic expects $E_{1000}(65,41)=8.0$ cycles of this type, and there are $9$.

\paragraph{A birthday factor} For $41\le l\le66$ the heuristic overestimates the counts, and increasingly so with $l$ (Figure~\ref{fig:cycles}). Over the $80$ types with $|D|>1000$ and $E_{1000}\ge1$ it predicts $206.3$ primitive cycles, and we observe $146$, a ratio of $0.71$ against $0.95$ for $l\le40$. The deficit is not confined to the algebraic factors: the coprime types give $0.84$. Nor is it a boundary effect: requiring the smallest value of $z_3$ over the rotations of a word to be at least $|D|/e$ changes the prediction by less than $1\,\%$, as sampling shows. What the heuristic ignores is that the $k$ odd members of a cycle are distinct. A random map on $N$ points has on average $\frac1k\prod_{i<k}(1-i/N)\approx\frac1k\,e^{-k^2/2N}$ cycles of length $k$; the factor $e^{-k^2/2N}$ is the probability that $k$ random points are distinct, and the heuristic takes it to be $1$. The odd members of a cycle of type $(l,k)$ of $3x+c_0$ have a typical size: if they were all equal to $x$, then $(3+c_0/x)^k=2^l$, that is, $x=M_e=e/|2^{l/k}-3|$ for $e=|c_0|$. With $N=\alpha M_e/2$, the number of odd integers up to $\alpha M_e$, each divisor gets the birthday factor:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:birthday}
E^*_C(l,k)=\frac{L(l,k)}{|D|}\sum_{e\,\mid\,|D|,\ e\le C}\varphi(e)\exp\Bigl(-\frac{k^2\,|2^{l/k}-3|}{\alpha\,e}\Bigr),
\end{equation}
and $E^*_C=E_C$ for the exact types with $|D|\le C$. The factor is close to $1$ near the line $l=k\log_23$ and for large $e$; it suppresses long words with small denominators. Fitted on the $116$ types with $l\le40$ by Poisson deviance, $\alpha=6$, and $E^*_{1000}$ predicts $833.3$ cycles there against $839$ observed. Out of sample, it predicts $163.4$ cycles for the $80$ types with $41\le l\le66$ against $146$ observed (ratio $0.89$), and the Poisson deviance on these types drops from $125.5$ to $99.8$. Corrections without this structure, fitted in the same way, do worse: a factor $e^{-bl}$ or $e^{-bk}$, or the size term alone, $\exp(-k|2^{l/k}-3|/(ae))$, give $114.5$, $117.8$ and $112.4$. Summed over all types with $l\le66$, $E^*_{1000}$ gives $1296.7$ primitive cycles against $1296$ observed, and $E_{1000}$ gives $1422.5$ (Figure~\ref{fig:cycles}); fitted on all lengths, $\alpha=4.5$. After the correction, the coprime types with $41\le l\le66$ give $0.99$ times the expected count. The even types with $D>0$ keep a deficit ($0.86$), which points to the algebraic factor $P$ again, and the lengths $49\le l\le56$ remain low ($0.65$).

\begin{problem}
\label{prob:heuristic}
Why does the equidistribution heuristic predict the number of primitive cycles of the types with $\gcd(l,k)=1$ so well, and can the birthday factor in~\eqref{eq:birthday}, with its constant $\alpha$, be derived? What explains the residual factor $0.86$ after Lemma~\ref{lem:halfword} for the algebraic factor $P$ when $D>0$ and the low counts for $49\le l\le56$, and is the remaining underdispersion of the counts real?
\end{problem}

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig1_cycles_vs_heuristic.pdf}
\caption{Primitive cycles of all maps $3x+c_0$ with $|c_0|\le1000$ per word length $l\le66$: census (bars; Run~A for $l\le40$, Run~P for $41\le l\le66$), heuristic $\sum_k E_{1000}(l,k)$ (solid line) and the same with the birthday factor~\eqref{eq:birthday}, $\alpha=6$ (dashed). The peaks at $l=19$ and $l=22$ come from $D(19,12)=-7153=-23\cdot311$ and $D(22,14)=-5\cdot7\cdot11^2\cdot139$.}
\label{fig:cycles}
\end{figure}

%=====================================================================
\section{Measuring forests: basin densities}
\label{sec:basins}
%=====================================================================
For a cycle $\mathcal C$ of $T_{q,c}$, its \emph{basin} is $\beta(\mathcal C)=\{n\in\N: T^j(n)\in\mathcal C \text{ for some } j\}$, and we write $d_N(\mathcal C)=|\beta(\mathcal C)\cap[1,N]|/N$. The basins are the trees of the forest of Section~\ref{sec:local}; the local structure fixes only the total density of all trees. We report empirical shares $d_N$. In general, the existence of the natural density $\lim_{N\to\infty}d_N$ is open; Propositions~\ref{prop:filtration} and~\ref{prop:cosets} establish densities for certain invariant classes, not automatically for individual basins. Theorem~\ref{thm:logdens} below establishes the logarithmic density of every basin in the stated family; a natural-density result for $3x+1$ follows using a recent result of Mazur (Corollary~\ref{cor:natural}).

Two empirical entries of Table~\ref{table:basins}, $0.20000$ and $0.07692$, are close to $1/5$ and $1/13$, respectively. The following elementary gcd invariance explains the scaling underlying these values, but their exact equality to those fractions would additionally require that the $3x+1$ basin of $1$ have density one. The invariance is implicit in Lagarias' primitive cycles \cite{Lagarias_1990}, explicit in \cite[(19)]{Belaga_Mignotte_DMTCS_2006} and also used by Wegner \cite{Wegner_2021}.

\begin{proposition}[Divisibility filtration \cite{Lagarias_1990,Belaga_Mignotte_DMTCS_2006,Wegner_2021}]
\label{prop:filtration}
Let $\gcd(c,6)=1$ and, for a divisor $e$ of $|c|$, let $B_e=\{n\ge1:\gcd(n,c)=e\}$. Then $T_{3,c}(B_e)\subseteq B_e$, the map $n\mapsto n/e$ conjugates $T_{3,c}$ on $B_e$ to $T_{3,c/e}$ on the integers coprime to $c/e$, and $B_e$ has natural density $\varphi(|c|/e)/|c|$.
\end{proposition}

Indeed, every prime divisor of $c$ is at least $5$, so that $T_{3,c}$ preserves $\gcd(n,c)$, and $T_{3,c}(em)=e\,T_{3,c/e}(m)$ for odd $e$.

Consequently, for every cycle $\mathcal C$ of $3x+c/e$ coprime to $c/e$, the basin of $e\,\mathcal C$ in $3x+c$ is the dilation by $e$ of the basin of $\mathcal C$ in $3x+c/e$. Its logarithmic density is therefore exactly $1/e$ times the logarithmic density of the smaller basin; the same scaling holds for natural densities whenever they exist. Proposition~\ref{prop:filtration} determines the densities of the gcd classes $B_e$, whereas an individual basin may occupy only a portion of its class. If $B_1$ contains a single known cycle, that basin exhausts $B_1$ only if all other orbits in $B_1$ form a set of density zero (for the chosen notion of density). In particular, writing $\beta_c(a)$ for the basin of the cycle through $a$ under $T_{3,c}$, we have the unconditional logarithmic-density identities
\[
\delta(\beta_5(5))=\tfrac15\,\delta(\beta_1(1)),\qquad
\delta(\beta_{13}(13))=\tfrac1{13}\,\delta(\beta_1(1)).
\]
The empirical values $1/5$ and $1/13$ become exact individual-basin densities if $\delta(\beta_1(1))=1$; the corresponding claim for natural densities likewise requires natural-density one for that basin.

The divisibility filtration has a multiplicative refinement. For $c$ coprime to $6$, let $G=(\mathbb Z/c\mathbb Z)^\times$ and let $H$ be the subgroup of $G$ generated by $2$ and $3$.

\begin{proposition}[Multiplicative filtration]
\label{prop:cosets}
Let $c\ge1$ with $\gcd(c,6)=1$. For every coset $gH$, the set $B_{1,gH}=\{n\in\N:\gcd(n,c)=1,\ n\bmod c\in gH\}$ is completely invariant under $T_{3,c}$, and it has natural density $\varphi(c)/(c\,[G:H])$. In particular, if $p\equiv\pm1\pmod{24}$ is prime, the Legendre symbol $(n/p)$ is constant along every orbit of $T_{3,p}$ on the integers prime to $p$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For $n$ prime to $c$, $T_{3,c}(n)$ is congruent to $2^{-1}n$ or to $3\cdot2^{-1}n$ modulo $c$, and both factors lie in $H$. Conversely, every preimage of an integer prime to $c$ is prime to $c$. Hence $B_{1,gH}$ is completely invariant. It is the union of $|H|$ residue classes modulo $c$, so its density is $|H|/c=\varphi(c)/(c\,[G:H])$. For a prime $p\equiv\pm1\pmod{24}$, both $2$ and $3$ are quadratic residues modulo $p$, so $H$ consists of quadratic residues.
\end{proof}

The proposition applies within every gcd class $B_e$ as well, with $c/e$ in place of $c$. The density $\varphi(c)/(c\,[G:H])$ belongs unconditionally to the invariant \emph{coset set}. Even if a coset contains only one known cycle, the basin of that cycle need not exhaust the coset: other cycles or nonconvergent orbits have not been excluded. Its basin has the full rational coset density only if the complement within the coset has density zero. The invariance is elementary and closely related to the Markov chains on residue classes of generalized Collatz maps studied by Matthews \cite{Matthews_2010}; we have not found it stated for the maps $3x+c$. The predicted coset shares are confirmed numerically below.

\begin{proposition}[Verification]
\label{prop:verification}
For $3x-1$ and the $17$ maps $3x+c$ with $0<c<50$ and $\gcd(c,6)=1$, every orbit of $1\le n\le10^{10}$ reaches one of the cycles of Proposition~\ref{prop:rational} or one of the three cycles with longer words found above (Run~B).
\end{proposition}

Figure~\ref{fig:basins} shows the resulting split of the forest for $3x-1$ and the $17$ maps $3x+c$ with $0<c<50$ and $\gcd(c,6)=1$. In the left panel, coloured segments are the basins of cycles coprime to $c$, labelled by their minima where space permits; grey segments are the basins of cycles of multiples of a divisor $e>1$ of $c$, whose densities are $1/e$ times basin densities of $3x+c/e$ by Proposition~\ref{prop:filtration}. For $c=7$, $19$, $31$, $41$ and $43$, the sampled integers coprime to $c$ all reached one observed cycle; the coprime class itself has exact density $(c-1)/c$, but equality with the individual basin density remains conditional on the absence of a positive-density exceptional set. The grey parts of $3x+25$, $3x+35$ and $3x+49$ reproduce the densities of $3x+5$ and $3x+7$, scaled by $1/5$ and $1/7$, to all printed digits. The densities are stable. The right panel follows the three basins of $3x-1$ through the dyadic windows $[2^j,2^{j+1})$, and in the window $[2^{32},2^{33})$ the densities of all basins differ from those up to $10^{10}$ by at most $2.4\cdot10^{-4}$. Within the cycles coprime to $c$, however, no pattern is visible. In $3x+5$, the cycle through $19$ attracts almost half of all integers, while the cycle through $23$ of the same type $(5,3)$ attracts less than a tenth. In $3x-1$, by contrast, the three basins are nearly equal, as already observed in \cite[Remark~1.7]{GGM_2025}. The type of a cycle, its length and number of odd steps, therefore does not determine its attractiveness.
